## Task

Calculate the neutral monoisotopic mass of the complete protein encoded by the CDS.

## Inputs

DNA inputs are 5′→3′ coding strands. Ignore whitespace between bases.

```json
{
  "cds": "ATGCTGGCAGATGCCGATGACGTGCTCGTGTAA"
}
```

## Rules

- Translate the CDS using NCBI genetic code 11, stopping at the first in-frame stop.
- Use an unmodified linear protein with both termini free. Keep the initiating residue and assume no disulfide bonds.
- Use the monoisotopic residue masses and include one water molecule for the complete peptide.

## Reference material

### Annotated CDS initiation

The annotated first codon of an intact CDS encodes initiating methionine (M), even when its internal-codon assignment is different.

### NCBI genetic code 11: internal DNA codons

Each internal DNA codon is an aligned group of three bases. The table gives its one-letter amino-acid assignment; STOP marks a stop signal.

```text
TTT=F  TTC=F  TTA=L  TTG=L
TCT=S  TCC=S  TCA=S  TCG=S
TAT=Y  TAC=Y  TAA=STOP  TAG=STOP
TGT=C  TGC=C  TGA=STOP  TGG=W
CTT=L  CTC=L  CTA=L  CTG=L
CCT=P  CCC=P  CCA=P  CCG=P
CAT=H  CAC=H  CAA=Q  CAG=Q
CGT=R  CGC=R  CGA=R  CGG=R
ATT=I  ATC=I  ATA=I  ATG=M
ACT=T  ACC=T  ACA=T  ACG=T
AAT=N  AAC=N  AAA=K  AAG=K
AGT=S  AGC=S  AGA=R  AGG=R
GTT=V  GTC=V  GTA=V  GTG=V
GCT=A  GCC=A  GCA=A  GCG=A
GAT=D  GAC=D  GAA=E  GAG=E
GGT=G  GGC=G  GGA=G  GGG=G
```

### Monoisotopic residue masses

Use the following incorporated residue masses in Da. A free linear peptide has the sum of its residue masses plus one H₂O, 18.010565 Da.

```text
A=71.037114  C=103.009185  D=115.026943  E=129.042593
F=147.068414  G=57.021464  H=137.058912  I=113.084064
K=128.094963  L=113.084064  M=131.040485  N=114.042927
P=97.052764  Q=128.058578  R=156.101111  S=87.032028
T=101.047679  V=99.068414  W=186.079313  Y=163.063329
```

- Round once at the end using unrounded intermediate values and the declared constants. Round exact halfway cases away from zero; rounded negative zero becomes zero.

## Reference material

### Annotated CDS initiation

The annotated first codon of an intact CDS encodes initiating methionine (M), even when its internal-codon assignment is different.

### NCBI genetic code 11: internal DNA codons

Each internal DNA codon is an aligned group of three bases. The table gives its one-letter amino-acid assignment; STOP marks a stop signal.

```text
TTT=F  TTC=F  TTA=L  TTG=L
TCT=S  TCC=S  TCA=S  TCG=S
TAT=Y  TAC=Y  TAA=STOP  TAG=STOP

CAT=H  CAC=H  CAA=Q  CAG=Q
CGT=R  CGC=R  CGA=R  CGG=R
ATT=I  ATC=I  ATA=I  ATG=M
ACT=T  ACC=T  ACA=T  ACG=T

GAT=D  GAC=D  GAA=E  GAG=E
GGT=G  GGC=G  GGA=G  GGG=G
```

### Trypsin cleavage specificity

rna.guide_hits/01 · format · 194.6s · $0.00656

Exact prompt

## Task

Find every coding-RNA window that matches the RNA guide within `max_mismatches`.

## Inputs

DNA inputs are 5′→3′ coding strands. Ignore whitespace between bases.

Coordinates and intervals are 1-based and inclusive unless a rule states otherwise.

```json
{
  "cds": "ATGGTGCATGACGTTGCGGAGGGCCAGGCCAAAGATTTGTGA",
  "guide": "GCCCUCCGCAAC",
  "max_mismatches": 1
}
```

## Rules

- Transcribe the CDS to obtain the coding RNA used in this task.
- Compare the reverse complement of the guide with every equal-length window of the coding RNA. The guide is supplied 5′→3′.
- For each window, count positions with different bases. Keep the window if that count is at most `max_mismatches`, including equality.
- Use ungapped comparisons and exclude wobble pairing. Include overlapping windows.

## Reference material

### Coding DNA to RNA

The supplied DNA is the coding strand, written 5′→3′. Copy its bases in the same order, replacing each T with U to obtain the coding RNA.

### RNA reverse complement

RNA complementary bases are A↔U and C↔G. Complement each base, then reverse the sequence to write its complementary partner 5′→3′.

## Answer

- `target_start` and `target_end` are 1-based inclusive coding-RNA coordinates. `mismatches` is the integer Hamming distance between that window and the guide’s reverse complement.
- List every qualifying window in increasing target_start order, including overlaps. An empty result is [].

Use exactly this structure. `<…>` marks a typed value; array items and map entries repeat as needed.

### Output structure

```text
[
  {
    "target_start": <positive integer>,
    "target_end": <positive integer>,
    "mismatches": <nonnegative integer>
  }
]
```

After any explanation, write `Final answer:` on its own line. Begin the raw JSON on the next line; it may span multiple lines and ends your response.

Expected answer

[{"mismatches":0,"target_end":24,"target_start":13}]

Final prediction

```json
[
  {
    "target_start": 13,
    "target_end": 24,
    "mismatches": 0
  }
]
```

## Task

Find a noncrossing RNA structure with the largest possible number of canonical Watson–Crick pairs.

## Inputs

DNA inputs are 5′→3′ coding strands. Ignore whitespace between bases.

```json
{
  "cds": "ATGAAGCGCGATGGCCTGCCAACGCAGATCGCGGCCGACGAGTGA",
  "min_loop": 3
}
```

## Rules

- Transcribe the CDS to obtain the coding RNA used in this task.
- Choose canonical Watson–Crick pairs with no nucleotide paired more than once.
- Pairs may be nested but must not cross: pairs `(i,j)` and `(k,l)` cannot coexist when `i < k < j < l`.
- Each selected pair must have at least `min_loop` sequence positions between its endpoints: `j - i - 1 >= min_loop`.
- Maximize the number of selected pairs, then apply the annotation tie-break below.

## Selection

Maximize the allowed pair count first. Among optimal structures, choose the ASCII-lexicographically smallest full dot-bracket string, comparing characters in the order opening parenthesis, closing parenthesis, then dot.

## Reference material

### Coding DNA to RNA

The supplied DNA is the coding strand, written 5′→3′. Copy its bases in the same order, replacing each T with U to obtain the coding RNA.

### Canonical RNA base pairs

The canonical Watson–Crick RNA pairs are A–U, U–A, C–G, and G–C.

### Dot-bracket notation

Matching parentheses mark paired RNA positions; a dot marks an unpaired position. Each closing parenthesis pairs with the most recent unmatched opening parenthesis.

## Answer

- Return one continuous dot-bracket string with one character per RNA base. Use parentheses for the selected noncrossing pairs and dots for unpaired bases.

After any explanation, write `Final answer:` on its own line. Put the required value or list on the next line to finish your response.

Expected answer

(((..(((((((..(.(((....)))))))))).)).)....)..

Final prediction

    "CG": 3,
    "GC": 3
  }
}
```

- Use the supplied position pairs as a fixed map. For each pair, look up the score of its two RNA bases in `pair_scores`, then add the scores.
- Return `INVALID` if any listed pair is not canonical or if any nucleotide position appears in more than one pair.
- Do not optimize the map or predict a different structure.

```

rna.t1_digest/05 · format · 114.2s · $0.00509

Exact prompt

## Task

Digest the coding RNA with RNase T1, allowing cuts according to `accessibility` and the supplied pairing map.

## Inputs

DNA inputs are 5′→3′ coding strands. Ignore whitespace between bases.

Coordinates and intervals are 1-based and inclusive unless a rule states otherwise.

```json
{
  "cds": "ATGGCGTCCTCCGACATCGGATACTGGATCCCGGTCGGGCTGACTCGCGCGGTGGCCAACATCTAG",
  "accessibility": "unpaired_only",
  "pairs": [
    [
      12,
      47
    ],
    [
      13,
      46
    ],
    [
      14,
      45
    ]
  ]
}
```

## Rules

- Transcribe the CDS to obtain the coding RNA used in this task.
- Apply the RNase T1 specificity rule with zero missed eligible cuts.
- With `accessibility=all`, ignore the pairing map when deciding where the enzyme can cut.
- With `accessibility=unpaired_only`, allow a cut only if the nucleotide recognized by the enzyme appears in no pair in the supplied map. Keep that map fixed after every cut.
- Return every nonempty fragment, including any uncut fragment at the end.

## Reference material

### Coding DNA to RNA

The supplied DNA is the coding strand, written 5′→3′. Copy its bases in the same order, replacing each T with U to obtain the coding RNA.

### RNase T1 cleavage specificity

RNase T1 cleaves RNA after G. That G remains on the upstream fragment.

## Answer

- `start` and `end` are 1-based inclusive positions in the supplied CDS-derived RNA; `fragment` is the continuous uppercase RNA sequence.
- Return every nonempty fragment in 5′→3′ order, retaining repeats and accounting for every base exactly once. An empty RNA gives [].

Use exactly this structure. `<…>` marks a typed value; array items and map entries repeat as needed.

### Output structure

```text
[
  {
    "start": <positive integer>,
    "end": <positive integer>,
    "fragment": <RNA string>
  }
]
```

After any explanation, write `Final answer:` on its own line. Begin the raw JSON on the next line; it may span multiple lines and ends your response.

## Task

Digest the coding RNA with RNase T1, allowing cuts according to `accessibility` and the supplied pairing map.

## Inputs

DNA inputs are 5′→3′ coding strands. Ignore whitespace between bases.

Coordinates and intervals are 1-based and inclusive unless a rule states otherwise.

```json
{
  "cds": "GTGTTCCTTTTGAGAGTGGTAATCGCAGAGGATCTTGAAACTTTCGTTCTCAGGGCAAGGTGA",
  "accessibility": "unpaired_only",
  "pairs": [
    [
      4,
      39
    ],
    [
      5,
      38
    ],
    [
      6,
      37
    ]
  ]
}
```

## Rules

- Transcribe the CDS to obtain the coding RNA used in this task.
- Apply the RNase T1 specificity rule with zero missed eligible cuts.
- With `accessibility=all`, ignore the pairing map when deciding where the enzyme can cut.
- With `accessibility=unpaired_only`, allow a cut only if the nucleotide recognized by the enzyme appears in no pair in the supplied map. Keep that map fixed after every cut.
- Return every nonempty fragment, including any uncut fragment at the end.

## Reference material

### Coding DNA to RNA

The supplied DNA is the coding strand, written 5′→3′. Copy its bases in the same order, replacing each T with U to obtain the coding RNA.

### RNase T1 cleavage specificity

RNase T1 cleaves RNA after G. That G remains on the upstream fragment.

## Answer

- `start` and `end` are 1-based inclusive positions in the supplied CDS-derived RNA; `fragment` is the continuous uppercase RNA sequence.
- Return every nonempty fragment in 5′→3′ order, retaining repeats and accounting for every base exactly once. An empty RNA gives [].

Use exactly this structure. `<…>` marks a typed value; array items and map entries repeat as needed.

### Output structure

```text
[
  {
    "start": <positive integer>,
    "end": <positive integer>,
    "fragment": <RNA string>
  }
]
```

After any explanation, write `Final answer:` on its own line. Begin the raw JSON on the next line; it may span multiple lines and ends your response.

## Task

Calculate CAI using the supplied codon weights.

## Inputs

DNA inputs are 5′→3′ coding strands. Ignore whitespace between bases.

```json
{
  "cds": "ATGTACGGTAACAACGGAAAACGTATGCCTGACCGCCCGGAGATGGATGATTTGAATGTTTCTTAA",
  "weights": {
    "GAC": 1.0,
    "GAT": 0.4,
    "GAA": 1.0,
    "GAG": 0.6,
    "GGA": 0.4,
    "GGC": 0.4,
    "GGG": 1.0,
    "GGT": 0.8,
    "AAA": 0.8,
    "AAG": 1.0,
    "CTA": 0.6,
    "CTC": 0.4,
    "CTG": 0.8,
    "CTT": 1.0,
    "TTA": 0.6,
    "TTG": 0.4,
    "ATG": 1.0,
    "AAC": 0.6,
    "AAT": 1.0,
    "CCA": 1.0,
    "CCC": 0.2,
    "CCG": 0.6,
    "CCT": 0.8,
    "AGA": 0.4,
    "AGG": 0.2,
    "CGA": 0.8,
    "CGC": 0.8,
    "CGG": 0.8,
    "CGT": 1.0,
    "AGC": 0.6,
    "AGT": 0.8,
    "TCA": 0.2,
    "TCC": 1.0,
    "TCG": 0.4,
    "TCT": 0.6,
    "GTA": 0.6,
    "GTC": 1.0,
    "GTG": 0.8,
    "GTT": 0.8,
    "TAC": 0.4,
    "TAT": 1.0
  }
}
```

## Rules

- Calculate the geometric mean of the supplied codon weights, excluding the initiator and terminal stop.

## Reference material

### Codon length

A codon is a group of three consecutive nucleotides.

### Codon adaptation index

For n included codons with supplied positive weights w₁,…,wₙ, CAI = (w₁ × … × wₙ)^(1/n), the geometric mean of those weights.

## Answer

- Return the CAI as a decimal number with 6 digits after the decimal point.
- Round once at the end using unrounded intermediate values and the declared constants. Round exact halfway cases away from zero; rounded negative zero becomes zero.

After any explanation, write `Final answer:` on its own line. Put the required value or list on the next line to finish your response.

## Task

Find every full-length window whose GC fraction falls outside `gc_bounds`.

## Inputs

DNA inputs are 5′→3′ coding strands. Ignore whitespace between bases.

Coordinates and intervals are 1-based and inclusive unless a rule states otherwise.

GC bounds are inclusive fractions. A string such as `"1/3"` denotes that exact rational value.

```json
{
  "cds": "ATGATCAACGAAGGTACACTGCGTCACAATGCAGGCAAGCCGACTGTTGCTAATTTATGA",
  "window": 9,
  "gc_bounds": [
    "1/3",
    "2/3"
  ]
}
```

## Rules

- Move a window of length `window` along the full CDS one base at a time. Use only windows that fit completely within the sequence.
- A fraction equal to either bound is allowed. Report starts only when the fraction is below the lower bound or above the upper bound.
- Compare against the exact bounds before rounding.

## Reference material

### GC fraction

GC fraction is the number of G and C bases divided by the number of bases in the sequence being measured.

## Answer

- Return a list of all qualifying 1-based integer start positions in increasing order, including overlapping windows. An empty result is [].

After any explanation, write `Final answer:` on its own line. Put the required value or list on the next line to finish your response.

## Task

Track the change in reading-frame phase after each insertion or deletion, and report the final phase.

## Inputs

DNA inputs are 5′→3′ coding strands. Ignore whitespace between bases.

Coordinates and intervals are 1-based and inclusive unless a rule states otherwise.

In an edit object, `replace` is `[first_base, last_base, new_text]`; an empty `new_text` deletes that interval. `insert_after` gives an original base boundary and `sequence` gives the inserted text. Apply all edits to the original coordinates, simultaneously.

```json
{
  "cds": "ATGCTCATTCTGCTCCAGCGCCACTACATTGGCGTGGTGTCGTGA",
  "edits": [
    {
      "insert_after": 6,
      "sequence": "C"
    },
    {
      "replace": [
        12,
        12,
        ""
      ]
    }
  ]
}
```

## Rules

- Process edits in order of their first affected original position. An insertion after base k is processed at position k+1.
- If edits share a processing position, keep their original order in the input list.
- After each edit, compute total inserted bases minus total deleted bases, reduced modulo 3 to a value of 0, 1, or 2. A later edit may restore phase 0.

## Answer

- `edits` has one record per input edit, ordered by its first affected original base; an insertion after base k uses k+1. Break equal-coordinate ties by the original input index.
- `edit_index` is the edit’s 1-based index in the input list. `phase` is cumulative (inserted bases minus deleted bases) modulo 3 immediately after that edit.
- `final_phase` is the last phase, or 0 for an empty edit list.

Use exactly this structure. `<…>` marks a typed value; array items and map entries repeat as needed.

### Output structure

```text
{
  "edits": [
    {
      "edit_index": <positive integer>,
      "phase": <integer: 0, 1, or 2>
    }
  ],
  "final_phase": <integer: 0, 1, or 2>
}
```

After any explanation, write `Final answer:` on its own line. Begin the raw JSON on the next line; it may span multiple lines and ends your response.

Expected answer

{"edits":[{"edit_index":1,"phase":1},{"edit_index":2,"phase":0}],"final_phase":0}

Final prediction

{
  "edits": [
    {
      "edit_index": 1,
      "phase": 1
    },
    {
      "edit_index": 2,
      "phase": 0
    }
  ],
  "final_phase": 0
}
